Plan of Care 
Barriers B1. Financial barrier 
Problems P1. Healthcare benefit information needed Goal: Healthcare benefit information understood(1)(2) 
Supporting evidence, suggestions, and alternatives


Interventions: RECONCILE: Benefits from multiple sources, if needed ASSIST: Identifying available financial coverage for current and potential future healthcare services EDUCATE: Types of healthcare coverage (as applicable for patient's situation)(3): 

Dental
 benefits/coverage - health plan payment for dental services (eg, annual examination or fillings)[A] 


Catastrophic
 health plan - health plan that covers medical care for catastrophic health needs (eg, tetraplegia (quadriplegia)) but requires the patient to cover all medical costs up to a certain amount (deductible) 



Children's Health Insurance Program (CHIP) - health insurance program for low-income children (and possibly pregnant women or families that do not qualify for Medicaid) 

Flexible
 spending account (FSA) - an employer-offered savings account that an employed patient can put money into before paying taxes on that income. The employee usually can decide how much money to put into the account. Money in the account has to be spent for qualified healthcare expenses, and usually has to be spent by the end of a certain period (eg, a calendar year). 


Health
 saving account (HSA) - a savings account into which an employee with a high-deductible health plan can put money before paying taxes on that income. Money in the account has to be spent for qualified healthcare expenses, and usually has to be spent by the end of a certain period (eg, a calendar year). 



In/out of network - some health plans have networks of care providers and offer reduced out-of-pocket expenses to patients who use providers in the network. In some cases, health plans may not cover any costs if patients seek care from out-of-network providers. 

Long-term care insurance - financial coverage for custodial care services 

Medicare
 Part A - Medicare financial coverage primarily for hospital-related expenses, or services in lieu of hospitalization (eg, DME) 


Medicare
 Part B - Medicare financial coverage primarily for outpatient care-related expenses 


Medicare
 Part C - type of Medicare offered through a commercial health plan to provide all of a patient's Medicare Part A and B benefits 


Medicare
 Part D - Medicare financial coverage of prescription medications for patients who enroll in the program 


Medicare
 Part F - Medicare supplement (Medigap) financial coverage for coinsurance and deductibles under Part A and B, Part B preventive care coinsurance, Part A hospice coinsurance, SNF coinsurance, and foreign travel medical care 


Medicare
 Part G - Medicare supplement (Medigap), coverage the same as Part F except does not cover Part B deductible 


Medicare
 Part K - Medicare supplement (Medigap), financial coverage for coinsurance costs of Medicare Part A up to an additional 365 days once Medicare benefits have been used. It covers at 50% Part B coinsurance and co-pays, Part A hospice coinsurance and co-pays, SNF coinsurance, and Part A deductible. 


Medicare
 Part L - Medicare supplement (Medigap), financial coverage as for Part K but at 75% 


Medicare
 Part M - Medicare supplement (Medigap), financial coverage for coinsurance costs of Medicare Part A up to an additional 365 days once Medicare benefits have been used, Part B co-pays and coinsurance, Part A hospice co-pays and coinsurance, SNF coinsurance, and 50% of Part A deductible. It also covers foreign travel emergency expenses (up to plan limit) and Part B preventive services. 


Medicare
 Part N - Medicare supplement (Medigap), financial coverage for Part A coinsurance, Part B co-pays and coinsurance (with an out-of-pocket limit), Part A hospice co-pays and coinsurance, SNF coinsurance, Part A deductible, foreign travel emergency expenses, and Part B preventive care 


Medicaid
 - health insurance program offered by states and intended for people with low incomes or disabilities 



Private/commercial insurance - financial coverage for healthcare costs by patient's participation in a private company insurance program 

State
 Health Insurance Assistance Program (SHIP) - a state-provided healthcare program to provide free healthcare coverage counseling for patients with Medicare 


TRICARE
 - financial coverage for healthcare services for active-duty or retired military personnel and their families 


Veterans 
Affairs (VA) benefits - financial coverage for healthcare costs for patients who have appropriate military service 


Vision
 benefits - health plan payment for vision-related services (eg, annual examination, corrective lenses)[B] 


Waiver
 programs - cover services for the patient to allow them to stay in the community rather than a nursing facility. Coverage is determined on a case-by-case basis, with availability and allowed coverage based on state allowance.(4) 



Workers' compensation - financial coverage by employers for healthcare services related to illness or injury 
occurring on the job EDUCATE: Benefit terminology: 

Annual
 limit - a maximum amount that a health plan will pay for the patient's healthcare in a year. It is often imposed for types of healthcare services (eg, medications or hospitalizations). Once the annual limit is met, the patient will be responsible for all costs of that care. 


Benefit
 year - what the patient's health plan will pay for in a year. The benefit year is usually the calendar year. 


Coinsurance
 - refers to the patient's percentile share of the costs for their healthcare service. For example, if the coinsurance is 20% for a $100 provider appointment, the patient is responsible for $20. 


Copayment
 - a set payment amount that the patient is responsible for when getting a healthcare service. This payment is made by the patient every time they use the service. For example, the patient may pay $20 for every appointment. 


Cost
 sharing - the patient's share of the healthcare costs; it generally refers to any type of out-of-pocket expense (eg, coinsurance or copayment). Patients may be responsible for any combination of the costs of their care. 


Deductible
 - the amount of money the patient must pay for healthcare services before the insurance company will start to pay. For example, if the patient's deductible is $1000, then the patient is responsible for the first $1000 of healthcare costs. Once the $1000 is paid, then the health plan starts to pay for care. Deductibles may not apply to all healthcare services; for example, the patient may have a deductible for hospital care but not for provider appointments. 


Excluded
 services - healthcare services that the patient's health plan does not pay for 


Lifetime
 limit - a limit on the total amount of healthcare costs a health plan will pay for a patient's healthcare. For example, there may be a total lifetime limit of $500,000 over the course of a patient's life. 



More
 information can be found at www.healthcare.gov. EDUCATE: Financial planning options (eg, payment programs) EDUCATE: How to save on out-of-pocket costs: 


Use
 in-network providers. 


Ask
 provider if any previsit testing can be done (to save on need for return visit). 




Health
 Marketplace Silver members can save based on income. COORDINATE: Submitting status report information on patient's condition to appropriate payer, if needed COORDINATE: Contact with providers if patient cannot afford aspect of care COORDINATE: Social worker referral, as needed COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed COORDINATE: Financial counseling or assistance COORDINATE: Follow-up to evaluate understanding of healthcare benefits (eg, coverage or access), referral status, and need for further assistance(5)(6) 


P2. Potential complication: financial issues present Goal: Financial resources identified and sufficient to continue care(2)(7) 
Supporting evidence, suggestions, and alternatives


Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying healthcare benefits coverage from payers ASSIST: Identifying availability of and eligibility for alternative financial resources ASSIST: Communicating information on patient's healthcare benefits coverage with treatment team ASSIST: Appointment scheduling and completing paperwork to support applications for financial support EDUCATE: Healthcare benefits and out-of-pocket expenses EDUCATE: Resources for financial assistance: 

Administration
 for Children & Families: www.acf.hhs.gov 


Centers
 for Medicare and Medicaid Services (CMS): www.medicare.gov/drug-coverage-part-d

Assistance Tool: https://medicineassistancetool.org 



NeedyMeds. Find help with the cost of medicine: www.needymeds.org

 pharmaceutical company websites COORDINATE: Financial assistance referral; examples include:

Goal: Financial issues addressed(7) 

Interventions: 
RECONCILE: Benefits from multiple sources, if needed 
ASSIST: Obtaining preauthorization from health plan provider, if appropriate 
EDUCATE: Source and type of financial support, how to access, and if follow-up needed 
COORDINATE: Follow-up for evaluation of use of support programs or ongoing need for further assistance(6)(9) 

Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should facilitate and follow up with referrals. Programs should identify patient barriers and adherence concerns.(10) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should have a process for identifying barriers to patients meeting goals or adhering to the case management plan and for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(10)

Complications 
Q1. Patient: What are the signs of bipolar disorder? (Check all that apply.) 

Aches and pains for no reason 

Changes
 in eating habits 


Crying
 spells for no reason 


Excessive
 risky behavior (eg, foolish business investments, sexual promiscuity, shopping sprees) 


Feeling
 tired, sluggish, or physically drained 


Feeling
 very irritable 


Feeling
 worthless or hopeless 


Feeling
 you can do things you cannot really do 



Hallucinations, like seeing things that are not there or hearing voices 

Jumping
 from one thought to another quickly 


Little
 or no interest in activities or hobbies 


Little
 or no interest in being with friends and family 


Manipulative
 behavior 


Poor
 personal hygiene, not wanting to bathe, or not caring about what you wear 


Talking
 very fast 


Thoughts
 of death, suicide, or harming self or someone else 


Trouble
 at school or work 


Trouble
 concentrating or remembering things 


Trouble
 making decisions 


Trouble
 sleeping or sleeping too much 


Very
 strong mood changes from being too happy or excited to being sad or feeling like everything is hopeless

Goal: Bipolar disorder complication risk evaluated and managed Interventions: 


ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling for evaluation of problems, comorbidities, and self-care plan[A]: 

Recently hospitalized patient's evaluation within 1 week of discharge 


Recently
 hospitalized patient's evaluation with outpatient healthcare providers (eg, nurse, social worker) within 1 to 3 days of discharge(3)(4)(5) 


Appointment
 every 3 to 6 months when patient is symptom free 



Appointment once a month or more for patient with questionable adherence to treatment plan EDUCATE: When and how to get help for worsening bipolar disorder problems EDUCATE: Follow-up appointments for assessment of clinical status[B](6) EDUCATE: Evaluation of condition status at least annually(7)(8) COORDINATE: Specialist referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Care team meeting, if needed(9)(10) COORDINATE: Follow-up to review progress with self-care plan, referral status, and need for further assistance(3)(11) RECONCILE: Communicate care plan changes to entire care team.(12)(13) 
P2. Bipolar disorder self-care information needed Goal: Bipolar disorder self-care information understood(14) 



Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) ASSIST: Obtaining a medical alert bracelet or necklace ASSIST: Support system (eg, family, friends) identification, enlistment, and education EDUCATE: Bipolar disorders are a group of mood disorders characterized by unusual shifts in mood, energy, activity levels, and ability to carry out day-to-day tasks. It is also called manic-depressive disorder.(15)(16) 

Changes in mood may be gradual or rapid, and vary by individual. It is a severe lifelong illness that often leads to functional impairment and disability.(15) 

It
 can cause impairments of personal, educational, social, and occupational functioning. EDUCATE: Self-care plan information(8)(15): 


Appointment
 scheduling and attendance 


Adherence
 to plan necessary to achieve goals, and complications of nonadherence (eg, exacerbations, progression, comorbidities)(6) 


Medication
 purpose, use, effects, and considerations (eg, timing before meals or exercise) 


Set
 small goals every day (eg, get out of bed at a certain time, finish one chore or project, or do something that brings pleasure like listen to music). 


Health
 checks, diary, and action plans (eg, write down thoughts, feelings, daily activities, eating times, and when medication taken) 


Lifestyle
 change plan (eg, psychotherapy, regular exercise, good sleep hygiene)(16)(17) 


Symptom
 management 


Relapse
 signs (eg, insomnia, decreased appetite, poor personal hygiene) 


Behavioral
 health monitoring and care (eg, depression monitoring and management) 


Stress
 management (eg, relaxation and coping techniques) 


Sleep
 hygiene (eg, regular pattern, calm sleep environment) 


Behavioral
 health treatment (eg, cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT), individual therapy, family therapy)[C] 


Caregiver
 support plan, as needed(8)(18) 


Crisis
 care plan: turn off distractions (eg, TV, radio), seek emergent care, call hotline, call police if escalates; caregivers advised to not argue(19) 


Safety
 awareness and management (eg, remove suicide risks, de-escalation techniques)(16)(20) 


Support
 group participation 


Comorbid
 condition management 


Diet
 considerations (eg, limit caffeine and alcohol) 


Exercise
 program within provider's recommendations(21) 


Medical
 alert bracelet/necklace, obtain and wear 


Laboratory
 monitoring, as needed 


Wellness
 checkups (eg, vaccinations, screenings) 




Tobacco
 cessation plan(7) EDUCATE: When to get emergency help(22): 





Suicidal or homicidal actions or plans(16)(20) 

Crisis
 plan not working 


Psychotic
 behavior (eg, threatening) 


Hallucinations
 or delusions, increased or new(16) 



Seizures, new or complicated (eg, seizure longer than 5 minutes, multiple seizures with loss of consciousness, head injury during seizure) 

Heart attack (eg, persistent chest pain; pain radiating to the arms, shoulders, neck, back, and/or jaw; cool clammy 
skin)(23) EDUCATE: When to call the provider for behavior changes(8)(22): 

Hallucinations or delusions increase. 

Self-harm (eg, abuse, mutilation) Insomnia

Self-care
 changes (eg, ADL or IADL problems) 


Self-care
 plan problems (eg, unable to follow, condition exacerbation) 



Substance
 misuse (eg, increased use, use interferes with ADL/IADL)(16) 


Appointments
 missed (eg, support group, provider) 


Cognitive
 changes (eg, thinking problems, forgetfulness worsens) 


Disorganization
 of thoughts (eg, flight of ideas, rapidly switching subjects in conversation)(22) 



Medication
 problems (eg, side effects, access, administration)(16) EDUCATE: When to call the provider(7):

 newly ordered from other providers

 or planning to get pregnant(7)

 gain or loss without reason COORDINATE: Home care referral, if indicated(12) COORDINATE: Crisis services referral, as needed COORDINATE: Lifestyle management program referral, as needed(16) COORDINATE: Telehealth referral, if indicated[D](24) COORDINATE: Tobacco use treatment, as needed COORDINATE: Chronic condition management referral, if indicated(24)(25) COORDINATE: Support group referral, as needed(24) COORDINATE: Vocational rehabilitation referral, as needed(8)(26) COORDINATE: Treatment program referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Social worker referral, as needed(24)(27) COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed(28) COORDINATE: Outpatient imaging, tests, or procedures, if indicated COORDINATE: Follow-up contact with patient to evaluate understanding and adherence to self-care plan, referral status, and need for further assistance(3)(11) RECONCILE: Self-care plans from involved care team members RECONCILE: Communicate care plan changes to entire care team.(12)(13)

De-escalation techniques (eg, caregiver remains calm and provides emotional support, do not argue or threaten, 
stay serious, announce actions beforehand)(15)(30) COORDINATE: Behavioral health services referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Psychiatrist referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Recreational therapy referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Protective services or legal authorities, as needed COORDINATE: Follow-up to evaluate status with behavior changes, treatment plan, referrals, and need for further assistance(3)(11)

P11. Anticonvulsant medication management needed 

Goal: Anticonvulsant medication initiated and maintained(15)(55) 

Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: Anticonvulsants may be used as mood stabilizers or for seizure treatment. EDUCATE: Call provider immediately: Vision problems, burning or tingling sensation, confusion, slowing of mental and physical ability, memory problems, speech problems, unusual fatigue, bleeding problems EDUCATE: Side effects:Appetite changes, tremors EDUCATE: Considerations: 

Obtain baseline platelet count and bleeding time before starting valproic acid. 

Valproic acid is not advisable for women of childbearing age due to the risk of birth defects. SEND: Anticonvulsant medicines

P12. Antidepressant medication management needed Goal: Antidepressant medication initiated and maintained(15)(56) 


Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose: Antidepressants may be indicated to help relieve symptoms of depression. 

Effects may take 2 to 8 weeks. 

Most people need to be on medication at least 18 to 24 months after adequate response to symptoms. EDUCATE: Call provider immediately: Suicidal ideation EDUCATE: Side effects: Sedation, dry mouth EDUCATE: Considerations: 

Augmentation
 agent may be added or change in antidepressant may occur if symptoms do not improve. 


Withdrawal
 symptoms may occur when discontinued.

P13. Atypical antipsychotic medication management needed Goal: Atypical antipsychotic medication initiated and maintained 


Interventions: ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with prescribing provider for medication evaluation and refills EDUCATE: Purpose:Atypical antipsychotics may be indicated for rapid control of acute mania to decrease agitation, aggression, and dangerous behavior.(57)(58) 

Second-generation antipsychotic medication EDUCATE: Call provider immediately: Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) (eg, extreme muscle rigidity, high fever, tachycardia, tachypnea, diaphoresis, stupor, fluctuations in blood pressure)[F](59) EDUCATE: Side effects: Difficulty urinating; jaundice; shuffling walk; fainting spells; seizures; pharyngitis; muscle spasms; excitement or restlessness; jerky movements of head, face, or neck; tachycardia; weight gain; blurred vision(60) 

Increased
 risk of metabolic side effects, including obesity, diabetes, and dyslipidemia(58) EDUCATE: Considerations(58): 


Use
 cautiously with history of seizures, liver disease, heart disease, blood disorders, or pregnancy.(61) 


Monitor
 blood glucose, cholesterol, and triglyceride levels. 




Talk

Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should ensure that during care transitions the care plan is re-evaluated, modified, and communicated to appropriate care team members (eg, providers, caregivers) as needed to support the care transition.(12)(68)(69) (70)(71) 
Quality standards: The American Case Management Association's Transitions of Care Standards indicate that patients' health risks should be assessed at each care episode or transition, a care management plan should be established and communicated across the care settings, and an evaluation should be done of the patient/family/caregiver's understanding of health status and self-care.(72) 
Quality standards: The American Case Management Association's Transitions of Care Standards indicate that processes should be in place to identify patients at risk for care transition problems, patients' health risks should be assessed at each care episode or transition, and a care management plan should be established and communicated across care settings.(72) 
Transition consideration: Several studies demonstrated a reduction in 30-day hospital readmissions when the patient follow-up was within 7 days of hospital discharge.(73)(74)(75) Earlier follow-up (eg, within 48 hours after hospital discharge) may also help reduce hospital readmissions for high-risk or vulnerable patients.(3) Follow-up appointment attendance rates were higher when appointments were scheduled before hospital discharge.(76)(77) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should develop and communicate an individualized case management plan with the patient, facilitate and follow up with referrals, and assess the patient's progress with the care plan. Programs should monitor and communicate the patient's progress with the care plan, modifying the plan as needed, and continue to identify patient preferences, barriers, and adherence concerns. Program content should include an individualized self-care plan that includes patient preferences, prioritized goals, interventions with information on whom to contact in urgent situations, and scheduled follow-up. Programs should facilitate communication and collaboration with practitioners in a timely manner.(68)(70)(71) 
Quality standards: The Case Management Society of America's Standards of Practice for Case Management principles include facilitation of informed decision making, counseling, and health education with patient self-care that is responsive to the patient's preferences (eg, culture, values, needs).(68) 
Quality standards: The Case Management Society of America's Case Management Adherence Guide includes guidance for improving adherence via patient engagement.(78) 
National guidelines: The 2018 Physical Activity Guidelines Advisory Committee advises that adults with chronic conditions or disabilities, who are able, should follow the key activity guidelines for adults. The guidelines recommend that adults should do at least 150 to 300 minutes a week of moderate-intensity or 75 to 150 minutes a week of vigorous-intensity aerobic physical activity, or an equivalent combination of moderate-intensity and vigorous-intensity aerobic activity. They should also do muscle-strengthening activities on 2 or more days a week. Older adults should do multicomponent physical activity that includes balance training as well as aerobic and muscle-strengthening activities.(79) 
10/10/24,10:59AM TC -Bipolar Disorder Evidence: A randomized controlled study based on 526 patients found that smoking cessation can be achieved among people with severe mental illness without negatively impacting mental health (eg, depression, anxiety).(80) 
Evidence:A retrospective cohort study of 17,629 patients found a significant reduction in healthcare utilization, hospital readmissions, and deaths when patients were discharged with home healthcare vs discharged to home without home healthcare.(81) 
Evidence:A retrospective cohort study based on 1151 Medicare accountable care organization (ACO) patients suggests utilizing a transitional care planning approach to prioritize discharging patients to the least restrictive next level of care, as patients discharged to skilled nursing facilities were almost 5 times more likely to be readmitted to the hospital at 30 days compared to patients discharged to the home healthcare setting.(82) 
National guidelines: The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) recommends offering supported employment 
programs to patients with bipolar disorder in primary or secondary care who wish to find or return to work.(8) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should have a process for identifying barriers to patients meeting goals or 
adhering to the case management plan and for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(68) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should have a process for identifying barriers to patients meeting goals or adhering to the case management plan and for following up with patients on the status of referrals. Programs should ensure that during care transitions the care plan is re-evaluated, modified, and communicated to appropriate care team members (eg, providers, caregivers) as needed to support the care transition.(68) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that caregiver resources should be evaluated. Programs should ensure that during care transitions the care plan is re-evaluated, modified, and communicated to appropriate care team members (eg, providers, caregivers) as needed to support the care transition.(68) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should have a process for identifying barriers to patients meeting goals or adhering to the case management plan and for following up with patients on the status of referrals. During transitions, medications should be documented with the patient and designee, and discrepancies should be communicated to the appropriate provider.(68) 
Outcomes: Several studies support the role of the pharmacist in medication reconciliation to identify and resolve pharmacy-related problems and improve outcomes (eg, prevention of medication-related adverse events, discontinuation of inappropriate medication). (83) 
Evidence:A prospective cohort study of 2655 hospitalized patients after discharge found that 24% of hospital medication changes were not followed, 30% of changed doses were filled with the incorrect dose, 27% of new medications were not filled, and 12% of discontinued medications were filled. Risk factors for medication change problems included increased out-of-pocket costs, not having medications filled before hospitalization, and discharge to a long-term care facility.(84) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should collaborate on transitions with the discharge team, reassessing the appropriateness as needed; communicate transition plan progress with the patient and designee; and communicate care plan information with the receiving setting within a designated time frame. Programs should develop and communicate an individualized case management plan with the patient, facilitate and follow up with referrals, and assess the patient's progress with the care plan. Programs should ensure that during care transitions the care plan is re-evaluated, modified, and communicated to appropriate care team members (eg, providers, caregivers) as needed to support the care transition.(68)

Footnotes 
[A] The timing of provider follow-up depends on the patient's disease severity, self-care complexity, patient/caregiver willingness and ability to provide patient care, and available social supports.(46) [ A in Context Link 1 ] 
[B] People with chronic behavioral health conditions have higher rates of medical illness and higher mortality vs the general population. 
(7)[ B in Context Link 1 ] 
[C] Electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) may be useful for severe and psychotic depression, particularly if the patient is not responsive to pharmacologic treatment.(8)(17)(85) [ C in Context Link 1 ] 
[D] Telehealth may improve access to and coordination of mental health care. Patients with behavioral health diagnoses may use telehealth or telepsychiatry to access mental health support or care (eg, to get assistance with problem-solving techniques or ask about presenting symptoms or medication use).(86) [ D in Context Link 1 ] [E] Referral to a behavioral health specialist is based on an established treatment plan and needs at discharge from inpatient care. A psychiatrist serves as the leader of the inpatient team and can perform psychotherapy, medication prescription, and other somatic therapies. A clinical psychologist conducts individual, group, and family therapy, and administers and interprets psychological testing. A psychiatric social worker conducts individual, group, and family therapy addressing a patient's social needs to include placement, financial support, and community requirements.(26) [ E in Context Link 1 ] 
[F] Neuroleptic malignant syndrome is a rare, rapidly progressive, potentially fatal complication more common with typical antipsychotics. Onset can occur hours or years after initiation of drug therapy.(59) [ F in Context Link 1 ]

Plan of Care 
Barriers B1. Limited or no caregiver resources 
Problems P1. Caregiver assistance needed Goal: Caregiver assistance identified and provided(1)(2) 


Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up 
ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) ASSIST: Identifying additional caregivers to offer support ASSIST: Identifying caregiver alternatives (eg, adult day care, community support groups, respite programs) EDUCATE: Importance of patient performing own self-care as much as they are able EDUCATE: Caregiver support(3): 

Identification of other caregivers 

Consideration of adult day care or respite program 

Self-care needs (eg, medical, behavior health, social support) EDUCATE: When and where to get help if caregiver support discontinued or insufficient COORDINATE: Home care referral, if indicated(4) COORDINATE: Respite care referral, as needed COORDINATE: Caregiver health service referral, as needed COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed COORDINATE: Support group referral, as needed COORDINATE: Social worker referral, as needed(5) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and monitoring caregiver status(6)(7) COORDINATE: Follow-up with patient and caregiver for evaluation of burden, status of referrals, identification of problems, and need for further assistance(8)(9)

Disease Management Quality Measures: 

Information provided from this question may be requested by various accrediting organizations. 
Plan of Care 
https://careweb.careguidelines.com/ed28/tc/tcp_04880.htm?printview=1 
Barriers B1. Health literacy barrier B2. Linguistic barrier B3. Cultural barriers B4. Hearing impairment B5. Vision impairment B6. Cognitive impairment present B7. Housing inadequate B8. Food insufficient B9. Financial barrier B10. Transportation inadequate B11. Limited or no caregiver resources B12. Limited community resources 
Problems P1. Language barrier present Goal: Language barrier resolved 



Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying translation options and using the most practical and appropriate one(2) COORDINATE: Call with patient and translation service and obtaining consent from patient to use translator(3)(4) ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) EDUCATE: Ways to overcome language barriers(2)(5): 

Speak
 slowly in normal tone. 


Discuss
 one topic at a time. 


Repeat
 and summarize often. 


Use
 names instead of pronouns. 


Use
 short, simple words and sentences. 



Use interpretation or translation services.(6) COORDINATE: Communication to providers to offer education materials in native language, whenever possible(6)(7) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(8)(9)(10) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to evaluate language barrier status, referral status, and need for further assistance 
SEND: What you need to know - Chronic condition management 
(Spanish) 
P2. Cultural barriers to healthcare present Goal: Cultural needs and practices incorporated into plan of care(6)(11)(12) 



Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) ASSIST: Identifying ways that cultural needs and practices can be incorporated into patient's plan of care(13) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with provider to evaluate incorporation of cultural preferences into self-care plan EDUCATE: Considerations of cultural practices on self-care, if appropriate COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed[A] COORDINATE: Cultural practices incorporated into care planning across providers COORDINATE: Home care referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Social worker referral, as needed(14) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(10) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to evaluate cultural practices and self-care plan, status of referrals, and need for further assistance(15)(16) 
P3. Hearing evaluation needed Goal: Hearing evaluated and self-care understood 



Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) ASSIST: Obtaining consent for communication with patient's caregiver for program continuation 
ASSIST: Appointment scheduling for hearing evaluation EDUCATE: When to get help for hearing problems (eg, infection, hearing problems get worse, social isolation, or depression)(17)(18) EDUCATE: Hearing loss implications (eg, safety risks)(17) COORDINATE: Audiologist referral, if indicated(17) COORDINATE: Home care referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Care team meeting, if needed(10)(19) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(8)(9)(10) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to evaluate hearing status, referral status, and need for further assistance(15)(16) 
SEND: What you need to know - Chronic condition management 
(Spanish) 
P4. Hearing impairment present Goal: Hearing maximized(20) Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) ASSIST: Obtaining consent for communication with patient's caregiver for program continuation ASSIST: Appointment scheduling for evaluation of hearing impairment(17) ASSIST: Informing patient's providers of hearing loss and preferred alternative communication method ASSIST: Identifying resources for hearing aids, phone lines, and other needed equipment and training ASSIST: Utilizing a sign language interpreter trained for medical settings who can assist during medical appointments, if appropriate ASSIST: Obtaining instructions in writing as much as possible(21) EDUCATE: Ways to work around hearing loss(17)(21): 





Hearing aid or assistive device 


Writing
 or drawing pictures 


Reading
 lips 


Technical
 aids (eg, TTY phone service or closed-captioning) 



Gestures
 or sign language EDUCATE: Hearing aids, benefit, use, and care, if appropriate(22) EDUCATE: Hearing loss implications (eg, safety risks)(22) EDUCATE: Ways to prevent further hearing problems(17)(22): 


Avoid
 places with loud persistent noises. 


Keep
 environmental noises down when trying to listen. 




Manage
 ear problems (eg, infection) quickly. EDUCATE: Ways caregivers can help with communication(17)(23)(24): 


Devote
 full attention when speaking or listening to patient, and avoid distracting environments (eg, those with competing noise). 


Face
 patient when speaking, talk slowly, and do not cover mouth. 


Ask
 patient to repeat back what they heard. 


Avoid
 jumping from topic to topic, and speak in simple sentences. 


Provide
 other ways to communicate (eg, offering paper for writing). 




Promote
 use of hearing aids, as appropriate.(25) EDUCATE: When to get help for hearing problems (eg, infection, hearing problems get worse, social isolation, or depression)(17)(25) EDUCATE: Resource: Hearing Loss Association of America: www.hearingloss.org COORDINATE: Otolaryngologist referral, if indicated(18) COORDINATE: Audiologist referral, if indicated(17) COORDINATE: Rehabilitation therapy referral, if indicated(26) COORDINATE: Home care referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Care team meeting, if needed(10)(19) COORDINATE: Communication between care team members and care plan reconciliation(8)(9)(10) COORDINATE: Follow-up contact to evaluate hearing status, referral status, and need for further assistance(15)(16)

Possible

Side
 effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. 


Common
 side effects with shot are sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and aches. 


Common
 side effects with nasal spray are mild and last 1 or 2 days; they include runny nose, headache, sore throat, and cough.

 reaction rare and is usually caused by a chicken egg allergy EDUCATE: Pneumococcal vaccination potential side effects: 


Side
 effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days. 


Common
 side effects include a sore, red, or swollen area where shot was given; fever; and muscle aches. 




Serious
 side effects are rare. EDUCATE: COVID-19 vaccination potential side effects(39):

 effects are mild and last 1 or 2 days.

Serious
 side effects are rare. COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed COORDINATE: Vaccinations COORDINATE: Follow-up to evaluate vaccination understanding, referral status, and need for further assistance(15)(16)

P11. Cognitive impairment present Goal: Cognitive and behavioral symptoms addressed and managed(42)(43) 


Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) ASSIST: Support system (eg, family, friends) identification, enlistment, and education ASSIST: Appointment scheduling for evaluation of new or worsening change in behavioral or cognitive function, and the development of a self-care plan ASSIST: Creation of a self-care plan between patient and provider (eg, goals, management, treating symptoms, getting help) ASSIST: Communication between multiple providers(44) EDUCATE: Cognition information(45): 

Cognition
 is ability to learn, solve problems, remember, and appropriately use information. 


Problems
 or deficits are part of aging process, but specific disorders may be treatable (eg, delirium, dementia, depression). 


Cognition
 deficits may be progressive. 



When
 and how to get help if cognition problems progress to point caregiver cannot provide care EDUCATE: Ways to help manage cognitive changes(45)(46): 


Agitation
 management (eg, simplify tasks and instructions, keep routine consistent) 


Maintain
 a calm environment. 


Set
 small goals every day (eg, get out of bed at a certain time, finish one chore or project, or do something that brings pleasure, such as listening to music). 


Structured
 activities (eg, exercise therapy, pet therapy, social contact) 


Focus
 on process instead of results. 


Break
 down all tasks into simple steps. 



ADL/IADLscheduled at times during the day when better able to perform or participate 

Encourage
 participation in ADL and IADL, but do not force. 


Organizational
 strategies and problem-solving tasks, including note-taking devices, cueing, and using memory books 


Mentally
 stimulating activities participation (eg, reading, puzzles) 




Orientation
 aids (eg, calendars, clocks, and alarms) EDUCATE: Medication side effects for cognitive and behavioral deficits (eg, antipsychotics, tricyclic antidepressants, SSRI antidepressants, dementia drugs) EDUCATE: Cognitive deficits may be progressive, and how to get help if they progress to point caregiver cannot provide care.

Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) ASSIST: Accessing information about advance care planning that is appropriate within state legal requirements(67) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling ASSIST: Identifying values and beliefs that influence end-of-life decisions(69) ASSIST: Identifying a healthcare proxy to make decisions when patient not able to ASSIST: Accessing and obtaining written physician orders for life-sustaining treatment that include the level of medical intervention to be delivered in the event of an emergency (comfort only, limited treatment, or full treatment) and use of artificial hydration and nutrition(67) ASSIST: Distributing advance care planning forms in collaboration with patient, family, and caregiver ASSIST: Using community resources for assistance with advance care planning forms, in coordination with patient, family, and caregiver ASSIST: Discussion on goals, values, and types and locations of care(69) EDUCATE: Advance care planning (ACP) information(28)(67): 

Documents
 patient's preferences/instructions for using, withholding, or withdrawing life-sustaining treatments and end-of-life care 


Treatment
 goals and postmortem care can be part of advance directives (ie, curative vs palliative care, burial vs cremation). 


Appoints
 medical decision maker for when/if the patient is unable to make own decisions 


Changes
 permitted, as preferences may change (eg, as patient ages or viewpoint changes) 


Types
 of ACP documents include living wills, healthcare/medical power of attorney (HCPOA or MPOA) 


Benefits
 of ACP: aligns patient's preferences with care plans and reduces treatment decision-making stress for caregivers 



States
 have different laws on what are considered acceptable/legal advance directives. Quality standards: Standards indicate that a needs assessment should be completed for every patient to identify and resolve potential communication barriers, including vision impairments, hearing impairments, and languages spoken other than English. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(81) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address psychosocial issues (eg, cultural practices) that may impede self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(81) 
National guideline: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force recognizes that age-related sensorineural hearing loss can adversely affect quality of life and functional abilities and is associated with increased risk of falls, hospitalizations, social isolation, and cognitive decline; however, there is insufficient evidence to assess the balance of benefits and harms of screening patients without symptoms of hearing loss.(82) 
Evidence: In a study of 99 adults (age 62 to 82 years), self-reported overall quality of life was significantly improved at 18 months after hearing aid fitting.(83) 
Evidence: Multiple studies conclude that hearing loss contributes to social isolation and mood disorders and is associated with increased rates of cognitive impairment.(17)(84)(85) 
National guideline: The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force recognizes that vision screening of older adults is important for the detection of age-related vision changes (eg, cataracts, refractive errors, adjustment to light intensity changes, distinguishing color, glare tolerance); however, there is insufficient evidence that vision screening in primary care for patients without symptoms of vision problems results in improved functional outcomes (eg, fewer falls).(86) 
Outcomes: Several studies support the role of the pharmacist in medication reconciliation to identify and resolve pharmacy-related problems and improve outcomes (eg, prevention of medication-related adverse events, discontinuation of inappropriate medication). (87) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that medications should be documented and any identified discrepancies communicated to the appropriate provider.(81) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address psychosocial issues that would impede self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(81) 
Evidence:A review of 4 independent studies with 14,749 low-income adults indicated that participants who were less healthy, had lower incomes, and had higher levels of stress and depressive symptoms were consistently more likely to have multiple social needs than those who were healthier and had higher incomes, lower stress, and fewer depressive symptoms. Women with children were less likely to need food, transportation, or money for necessities. Older, less healthy adults needed food, transportation, and money for necessities and unexpected expenses.(88) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address psychosocial issues (eg, inadequate or unsafe housing) that would impede self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(81) 
Evidence:A retrospective review of 22,324 medical records determined that 3 unstable housing circumstances (behind on rent in the past 12 months, 2 or more moves in the past 12 months, and a history of homelessness in the child's lifetime) are associated with adverse caregiver and child health status, developmental risk, and other household hardships.(89) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address psychosocial issues (eg, financial barriers) that would impede self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(81) 
Evidence:A retrospective study of 7959 nationally representative US children determined that household food insecurity is associated with higher emergency department use, higher school absenteeism, and lower access to care.(90) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address psychosocial issues (eg, transportation) that would impede self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(81) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should include a condition treatment plan and information on external resources; in addition, patient-provider communication should be encouraged (eg, with information on what to do before, during, and after a provider visit). Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(81) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that programs should have a process for identifying barriers to patients meeting goals or adhering to the case management plan and for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(81) 
Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address referrals to external resources that may support self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(81)

Footnotes 
[A] Community services may include cultural organizations that offer support.(12) [ A in Context Link 1 ] 
[B] Many driver rehabilitation programs provide therapy and behind-the-wheel assessments. Therapy assessment covers motor, cognitive, perception, and reaction times. The behind-the-wheel assessment covers fitness to drive.(91) [ B in Context Link 1 ] 
[C] In the United States, most states have legislation that delineates decision-making authority for patients without advance directives (a default surrogate), but there is a lot of variation in prioritizing, appointing, and the process for challenging default surrogates. In states that have a surrogate hierarchy, the priority is usually: spouse, adult child, parent.(67) [ C in Context Link 1 ]

Problems P1. Community resources needed Goal: Community resources identified and coordinated(1)(2)(3) 



Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling with provider for patient at risk of self-care problems or to support referrals to community services (eg, completing medical forms for transportation service) ASSIST: Identifying available community resources and contacting for support(4) EDUCATE: Types of community resources available(5) EDUCATE: Resources:

10/10/24,11:04AM TC -CommunityResources COORDINATE: Community services referral, as needed COORDINATE: Support group referral, as needed COORDINATE: Social worker referral, as needed(6) COORDINATE: Follow-up to evaluate community resources, referral status, and need for further assistance(7)(8) 

Quality standards: Standards indicate that program content should address referrals to external resources that may support self-management. Programs should have a process for following up with patients on the status of referrals.(9)

The Transitions of Care guidelines are designed to assess a patient's transition of care needs, provide evidence-based care planning, and promote condition self-management for patients with post-acute care needs. The Transitions of Care guidelines help healthcare professionals standardize and improve care coordination assessment and planning during the transition between care settings, while providing resource coordination, education, and advocacy. 
The Condition Self-Management guidelines provide a stratified approach to managing patients in transition. These guidelines are condition-specific and are for situations in which patients are likely to benefit from case management to ensure they understand their care needs and that these needs are met appropriately. The condition-specific guidelines are meant to support the population managed telephonically or in-person with full assessments and plan-of-care components. Each condition covered contains a comprehensive set of patient education materials. These materials are written in layperson language (fourth-grade to sixth-grade reading level) and may be sent or given directly to patients. 
Transitions of Care Overview 
The purpose of the Transitions of Care guidelines is to provide a supporting framework for programs that work to improve the transitions between care settings, prevent avoidable hospital readmissions, and promote patient self-management. Transitions of Care provides standardization of care planning for post-hospital and post-acute care transitions. 
Transitions of Care Title and Code 
Transitions of Care guidelines can be accessed directly from the table of contents page or via Search. 
For identification purposes, each guideline is assigned a "Guideline Code," for example, TC-0003. In this instance, the "TC" designates Transitions of Care. The numbers that follow the prefix are unique to this guideline, which is Asthma. 
Transitions of Care Guidelines 
ATransitions of Care guideline is comprised of assessment questions with answer selections and corresponding barriers, problems, goals, and interventions. Users can combine the guidelines to create a comprehensive assessment and care plan that focuses on the various elements of optimal patient care. For example, users may elect to include guidelines for preoperative care coordination, home care coordination, outpatient condition self-management, and home safety evaluation. 
Assessment Sets 
There are 7 assessment sets included in Transitions of Care: Enrollment, Pre-Hospital Planning, Hospital Transition Planning, Post-Acute Care Transition Planning, Outpatient, General, and Case Closure. 
Guideline Types 
Assessment sets include the following types of Transitions of Care guidelines to help users identify the scope or audience for a particular assessment: assessment and planning, record reviews, patient interviews, case management evaluations, level of care assessment tools, checklists, and follow-up. The assessment and planning guidelines address caregiver support and patient understanding of tasks to be completed (eg, evaluation or testing, medicine changes, level of care determination, or healthcare costs) and look for problems the patient may have with completing these tasks. Record review guidelines are intended to collect relevant transition planning information from available medical record sources. They help to ascertain the patient's location prior to admission, the status of advance care planning documents, and available healthcare coverage. Patient interviews are meant to be done during the direct patient (or appropriate caregiver) interaction and identify the patient's transition preferences, safety issues, knowledge, and psychosocial needs. Case management evaluations help the case manager identify transition needs, options, and issues to create safe transition plans. Level of care assessment tools address admission to home care or skilled nursing care and align with Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services' patient assessment instruments. The checklists are simplified guidelines to quickly identify problems with the functional elements of a transition plan. Follow-up guidelines help the user evaluate the patient's status, treatment plan, and understanding of follow-up activities (eg, follow-up appointments, medication changes, pharmacy/medical supplies or equipment, new therapies ordered, or laboratory work) and looks for problems the patient may have with completing these activities. 
Enrollment 
Three guidelines make up the Enrollment section: Evaluation for Case Management, Case Management Program Enrollment, and the Case Management Start of Care Assessment. The Evaluation for Case Management is a high-level guideline that helps users determine which patients are likely to need case management or care coordination support. The Case Management Program Enrollment guideline helps users establish eligibility for the case management program. It also provides information about case management that can be shared with the patient so they can make an informed decision about whether or not to enter the program. The Case Management Start of Care Assessment is a comprehensive evaluation of the patient's medical, behavioral, and psychosocial status, intended to help identify the patient's needs and prioritize care. 
Social Determinants of Health 
The Social Determinants of Health guidelines are assessments that can help identify the patient's needs and prioritize further assessment and care plan needs. These guidelines are comprehensive, covering elements of social, psychosocial, economic, and physical needs. This section can help healthcare professionals determine if the patient has access to sufficient caregiver, community, and financial support. Assessment and care-plan elements are provided to identify barriers (such as lack of health literacy) or social issues (such as transportation issues) that are adversely affecting the patient's ability to maintain optimal health. The Social Determinants of Health Screening Assessment included in this section provides a baseline to determine if the patient is experiencing food insecurity, housing concerns, or safety issues and is a starting point to build a plan of care that incorporates effective interventions and referrals. Resources and suggestions for referrals are provided at the end of the assessment and may vary by region. 
Pre-Hospital Planning 
When patients have a planned or elective procedure, proper planning can help ensure that the procedure is not delayed and that if post-procedure care is needed, that care is arranged according to patient preferences and early enough to prevent a delay in discharge. The Preoperative Care Coordination and Outpatient or Short-Stay Surgery guidelines are comprehensive assessments that can help identify the patient's needs and prioritize the assessments and care plans to ensure that all preoperative, outpatient, or short-stay care needs are met and transition plans are initiated. The Perioperative Care Coordination Patient Interview identifies the patient's knowledge of the procedure and psychosocial needs. 
Hospital Transition Planning 
The Hospital Transition Planning section has 3 major subsections: Initial, Concurrent, and Transition Coordination. The Hospital Transition Planning section provides assessments and care plan activities or interventions that the patient may need to ensure a timely, complete, and safe transition after acute care. 
Initial 
The Initial section is comprised of a Record Review and Patient Interview guideline. The user completes these guidelines as the initial step toward transitioning the patient to another level of care. 
Concurrent 
The Concurrent section is comprised of guidelines to support the preparation of a care transition plan and assist the user in evaluating how imminent the discharge may be. The Hospital Care Progression - Assessment and Planning guideline may help the user evaluate how well the patient is progressing toward discharge by identifying care needs, evaluating for potential discharge problems, and taking actions to ensure the patient gets needed care and initiate the transition plan. The Hospital Transition Management - Assessment and Planning guideline assists the user in collating information from the care team and determining primary and secondary transition plans that correspond to patient preferences, care team recommendations, and available resources. 
Transition Coordination 
The Transition Coordination section contains guidelines to support the logistical aspects of patient transitions between care settings and to support follow-up contact with patients to help identify potential care gaps. The Assessment and Planning guidelines in this section titled Post-Acute Skilled Care Facility Coordination, Home Care Coordination, Long-Term Care Facility Coordination, and Behavioral Health Coordination help the user take steps to facilitate a transfer to a care provider at a lower level of care and resolve issues that may cause transition problems, such as lack of benefits or insufficient provider availability. The Hospital Transition - Checklist provides the user with a complete checklist to ensure that discharge and transition activities have been completed, with corresponding care plan activities or interventions to address incomplete items. The Follow-Up guidelines help the user evaluate the patient's status (eg, follow­up appointments, medication changes, and new therapies ordered) after an emergency department visit, transition to home, or transition to post-acute services. 
Post-Acute Care Transition Planning 
The Post-Acute Care Transition Planning section has 3 major subsections: Initial, Concurrent, and Transition Coordination. The Post-Acute Care Transition Planning section provides assessments and care plan activities or interventions the patient may need to ensure a timely, complete, and safe transition after care with a post-acute provider. 
Outpatient 
The Outpatient section contains guidelines for case management activities when the patient transitions to community-based care (such as to their home with outpatient follow-up). 
Care Coordination Evaluation - Record Review: This guideline helps users collect key information from existing medical records to assess the patient's status, identify needs, and reconcile conflicts with the patient's self-management plan. 
Condition Self-Management: This section is comprised of condition-specific guidelines. Each guideline starts with an assessment that presents questions to identify what the patient knows about their condition, the ordered treatments, and the self-management plan. The assessment then guides the creation of a plan of care while supporting the patient's own self-management. Each guideline provides patient education interventions that help ensure the patient knows how to monitor for problems due to the condition, has an action plan should the condition worsen, and knows how to prevent the condition from causing problems (eg, by taking medication or following a diet). 
The assessment portion of the Condition Self-Management guidelines is broken into sections titled Patient Interview and Case Management Evaluation to facilitate the use of the guidelines at multiple points of engagement. The Patient Interview section is broken into subsections to allow users to focus on a particular aspect of the patient's self-management activities when they are engaging directly with the patient. These are: 

Self-Care: evaluates patient understanding of the activities the patient needs to do to monitor and manage their condition, such as blood pressure checks or weight monitoring 

Activity: evaluates what the patient knows about the activity appropriate for the condition and identifies potential issues affecting the patient's ability to stay active and exercise 

Nutrition: evaluates what dietary plans the patient is following 

Medicine: facilitates review of medications the patient may be taking for a particular condition and identifies problems the patient may have related to medication use or side effects 

Complications: identifies patient understanding or existence of potential complications and covers issues such as symptoms of diagnosis and what to do if symptoms occur 

Follow-Up Care: evaluates the plan and patient understanding of follow-up care activities (such as procedures, appointments, or laboratory work) and looks for problems the patient may have with completing these activities 
In the Case Management Evaluation section, the case manager can then review the patient's knowledge and reconcile it with the provider's treatment plan, thereby identifying care gaps or conflicts as well as patient education needs. 
Provider Appointment - Follow-Up and Urgent Care - Follow-Up: These guidelines help the user evaluate changes in the patient's care plan, along with the patient's understanding of these changes, after a recent provider appointment or urgent care visit. 
General 
The General section helps clinicians identify issues such as social determinants of health, home safety, medication reconciliation, hospitalization risk evaluation, and readiness to change. The guidelines are intended to be used at any appropriate point in the patient's care, such as after a doctor's appointment or following a hospital stay. 
Case Closure 
The Transitions of Care Case Management Closure guideline contains a brief assessment to help case managers document why a case is being considered for closure, as well as a plan of care to identify problems, goals, and interventions. 
Transitions of Care Format 
The interactive format (eg, Indicia for Care Coordination) makes it easier for healthcare professionals to move quickly through the assessment portion of guidelines and formulate the most appropriate goals and identify optimal interventions. Depending on how the user answers each question, the software automatically culls through the remaining questions and presents only those that are relevant. Once the assessment is completed, only those goals and interventions that address the problems identified are displayed. The interactive guidelines are fully documentable. In the static format, all questions, answers, barriers, problems, goals, and interventions are displayed where relevant in each Transitions of Care guideline. 
Assessment 
Many questions are written to solicit information from the patient (ie, are open-ended), using the principles of motivational interviewing, which allows the user to evaluate what the patient already knows vs leading the patient to a particular answer. The questions aim to identify problems so that appropriate goals and interventions can be established as well as to collect data for outcomes reporting. The answers include common answers that patients may provide on a particular subject. An "other" answer is available in multiple-choice questions to provide a place to document when an answer selection is not available. 
There are 3 audience types in the Transitions of Care guidelines to help users identify the person to whom the question is directed. If the question is intended to be asked of the patient directly, the question will have the delineation "Patient." The question will be delineated "Caregiver" when it should be asked of the appropriately designated family member or other person caring for the patient. This becomes important when the patient potentially has impairments that require support for their self-management. Case management-related assessment questions are tagged "Case Manager." These questions are intended for the case manager to evaluate the information collected and to make evaluations on next steps or priorities for care planning activities. The audience-type designation is for user reference only and not intended to be viewed by the patient or caregiver. As different users refer to their audience in multiple ways (eg, member, beneficiary, client), "Patient" is intended for identification of the audience only. 
Plan of Care 
The Plan of Care identifies barriers, problems, goals, and interventions that can help clinicians optimize the patient's care plan. 
Barriers 
Barriers are issues that would prevent the patient from participating in the program and that should be addressed before continuing with further assessment and care planning. Individualized barriers to goals are identified by answers to assessment questions. Barriers triggered by patient answers should be resolved before other care plan priorities are implemented. Barriers in content are often related to psychosocial needs and, until addressed, implementing a plan of care might not be effective. Consider referencing the Social Determinants of Health Screening Assessment as a first step in screening for social determinants of health needs. The identification of barriers provides the clinician with the information required to select the interventions needed to achieve the goals set by the patient and clinician. 
Problems 
Problems are generated when the answer to a particular question or set of questions identifies patient care needs. Problems include the need for more information about the condition or establishment of a self-management plan. 
Goals 
Goals are desired outcomes. They address deficiencies identified in the assessment. Goals include improved knowledge of the condition, better self-monitoring and management of the condition, effective medication management, and tobacco and alcohol abstinence, among many others. 
Interventions 
Interventions list the specific strategies and actions that healthcare professionals can take to help patients meet the identified goals. These actions fall into 5 categories of "Reconcile," "Assist," "Educate," "Coordinate," and "Send," which delineate the type of action the healthcare professional will perform. Reconcile interventions may prompt the clinicians to perform an activity during the plan of care (such as when a conflict with an order exists or resources are not available). Assist interventions are those the clinician will assist the patient in doing, with the patient leading the action. Educate interventions are the items the clinician can provide to educate the patient. Coordinate interventions are those the clinician will assist the patient in doing, with the nurse leading the action. Send interventions identify related items that the patient should be given. Clinicians may be prompted to perform interventions from several or all of these categories. For example, clinicians may be prompted to reconcile a medication list, assist the patient in obtaining a physician evaluation, educate the patient about symptoms to watch for, coordinate referrals to other healthcare providers, and send appropriate patient education materials. 

The Evidence Summary presents discussion and review of the evidence that supports the statements made in the guideline. The Evidence Summary summarizes the results of studies that also support a particular care plan activity or intervention, such as what treatments have been shown to have the greatest effect for patients with a particular condition or what interventions (such as tobacco cessation and exercise) can have a beneficial effect on wellness. The reviews, trials, series, reports, and analyses that form the evidence base are cited, summarized, and interpreted throughout the Evidence Summary and serve as the foundation for best practice recommendations for delivery of case management. 
Transitions of Care Patient Education 
The Transitions of Care guidelines provide educational materials written at a fourth-grade to sixth-grade reading level that can be given to the patient. Readability level is gauged using several readability tools, including Spache, Dale-Chall, Flesch-Kincaid, SMOG, and others. The patient handouts are available in PDF for easy printing and also in Spanish. Handouts include several diaries, such as a weight diary, that can assist patients in tracking their progress against goals. The evidence supporting the Patient Education materials may be found cited within the Plan of Care and Evidence Summaries of the Transitions of Care guidelines. 
Patient Education handouts are developed and designed to address the fact that patients have varying literacy skills through word choice, sentence construction, layout and design, and visual aids. The handouts are written in the active voice and in a positive tone, using second-person pronouns (you/your). Sentences are short and simple, with minimal use of compound or complex sentences. Words with 1 or 2 syllables are used whenever possible. Some exceptions include multisyllable medical and technical terms when they cannot be substituted with another word (eg, "oxygen," "glucometer"). The layout and design follow principles intended to make content easier to read and understand, such as using large amounts of white space, breaking content into short sections with simple headings, presenting complex ideas in easy-to-follow steps and lists, and using a font that is 12 point in size or larger. Many handouts contain icons to help readers understand the main topics of particular sections, such as a medicine bottle icon next to the header "Medicines" and knife and fork next to "Eating Plan." There are also illustrated handouts with step-by-step pictures that walk the patient through a particular care process or activity. 
Benchmarks and Data 
Benchmarks and Data for Transitions of Care provides information that organizations may use to compare their performance to that of other organizations based on a nationwide data sample. This includes condition-specific information on length of stay by level of care, number of home care visits, percentage of patients discharged from a hospital to a post-acute level of care (ie, recovery facility or home care), and hospital readmission rates. 
Benchmark Statistics Dashboard - Transitions of Care 
Transitions of Care guidelines are linked to a Benchmark Statistics Dashboard, which displays statistical information regarding utilization of services by patients falling under that guideline. The dashboards are accessed by a link marked "Benchmark Statistics" at the top of the guideline page. 
The dashboard presents a variety of measures that correspond to the companion guidelines, including use of acute hospital, inpatient rehabilitation facility, and skilled nursing facility; admission and readmission rates; and length of stay. 
The data are available for Medicare and Commercial populations, and benchmarks are shown for the total data sample, and for 9 multistate regions of the United States. 
For more detailed information on data sources, benchmarks, and methodology, please refer to the Methodological Notes tab within the dashboards. 
Abbreviations 
The Abbreviations resource lists common medical and care guidelines abbreviations and their corresponding definitions. This resource also provides definitions for units of measurement. 

Care Management Tools provide additional information on managing particular elements of care. These tools aid case managers and other healthcare professionals in creating and managing the treatment plan, and they help educate the patient and caregiver about treatment. Included in this section are various tools and calculators that address patient monitoring and assessment, and management of specialized types of care. The Care Management Tools section also provides definitions of levels of care across the continuum. 

The Quality Measures section describes the accreditation standards and performance measures that are addressed in the guidelines. The quality measures are intended to highlight areas of focus for major national quality initiatives or performance measures of national organizations. They are presented for reference purposes only and are neither an assurance of compliance nor a prescribed measurement set. The selection of accreditation organization, quality initiatives, measurements, and data collection instruments is at the user's discretion.

Plan of Care 
Problems P1. Depression complication risk present Goal: Depression complication risk evaluated and managed 



Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) ASSIST: Appointment scheduling for evaluation of problems, comorbidities, and self-care plan[A]: 

Recently
 hospitalized patient's evaluation within 1 week of discharge(1) 


Recently
 hospitalized patient's evaluation with outpatient healthcare providers (eg, nurse, social worker) within 1 to 3 days of discharge(2)(3)(4) 


Appointments
 for patient receiving nonpharmacologic therapies should occur several times in first 3 months to determine if therapy is helping to improve symptoms. 


Appointments
 for patient receiving pharmacologic therapy should occur within 2 weeks of initiating treatment, then every 2 to 3 months until patient is stable or in remission. More frequent follow-up may be appropriate for patients with high risk of suicidal ideation. 




Appointment once a month or more for patient with questionable adherence to treatment plan EDUCATE: When and how to get help for worsening depression problems EDUCATE: Follow-up appointments for assessment of clinical status EDUCATE: Treating depression can help improve outcomes of coexisting chronic medical conditions. EDUCATE: Treatment of coexisting chronic medical conditions may alleviate or eliminate depression.(5) COORDINATE: Specialist referral, if indicated COORDINATE: Care team meeting, if needed(6)(7) COORDINATE: Follow-up to review progress with self-care plan, referral status, and need for further assistance(2)(8) 
P2. Depression self-care information needed Goal: Depression self-care information understood(11) 



Interventions: ASSIST: Identifying patient's goal priorities, willingness/ability to achieve goals, and preferences for communication and follow-up ASSIST: Goal setting that is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and with target completion time (eg, short/long­term, prioritized) ASSIST: Support system (eg, family, friends) identification, enlistment, and education ASSIST: Obtaining a medical alert bracelet or necklace EDUCATE: Major depressive disorder, also known as clinical depression, is a debilitating mood disorder.(12) 

It
 is characterized by a pervasive and persistent sadness, despair, and diminished ability to think or make decisions. 


It
 is accompanied by low self-esteem and a loss of interest or the ability to experience pleasure. 


It
 is a common condition among patients with chronic medical illnesses. 


EDUCATE: Self-care plan information:

Set

Health
